activities quietly.\n  manifestation: At book club or during quiet evenings with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting quietly with the book or the movie, and before I know it I'm commenting, rustling in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I move from lesson planning to laundry to softball practice to chopping food for a community event without much pause, even on nights I promised myself I would rest.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I can take over a conversation in the Spanish department or at the cultural center, giving three examples when one would do, and then I feel embarrassed when I see people trying to wrap it up.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: In group discussions I often jump in before someone has finished their thought, especially at I'll go straight from lesson planning to... doing the laundry, then out to softball practice, or chopping food for a community event. I don't really pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest... I don't. I just keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things most days?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I mean, I have normal things to think about, but I wouldn't say I'm sitting around worrying about them. I just prefer to keep busy.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties with restlessness and staying seated start when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. Um... looking back, even as a girl, my report cards usually said I was bright, but... that I couldn't stay in my seat. Or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that I wore a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was ten. So... yes. It's not exactly new.\n\nDoctor: When you’re working on something, do you often have trouble keeping your attention on it?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. Um... if I have something to do, I just do it. I don't really have trouble focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose track of what you’re doing or make careless mistakes because your mind wanders?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I don't really make careless mistakes. Um... I keep track of what I'm doing just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act without thinking first, even when you know you probably should stop and consider it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I usually think things through. I don't just jump into things without considering them first.\n\nDoctor: When you’re sitting still, do you feel physically restless or like you have to move?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose a little. Um... if I'm sitting at my desk grading essays, or... in church, maybe. I'll catch myself tapping my foot, or clicking my pen. Just... shifting around. I usually just have to remind myself to settle down. But... that's about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling worried or anxious about many different things most days?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um... just the normal amount, I suppose. I don't really spend my days worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying close attention to details or make careless mistakes when doing tasks?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I have to pay pretty close attention to things, grading papers and all. So... no, I don't think I make careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have these restlessness and getting-up habits been present in more than one setting, like both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't really think so. Um... at home, I just go about my evening, so I haven't really noticed it there. Just... what I mentioned before. I just don't really stop. \n\nEspecially lately. Since my youngest left for college, the house has been a lot quieter, and I just find myself constantly doing something. I'll go straight from lesson planning to... doing the laundry, then out to softball practice, or chopping food for a community event. I don't really pause. Even on the nights I promise myself I'm just going to sit down and rest... I don't. I just keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things most days?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I mean, I have normal things to think about, but I wouldn't say I'm sitting around worrying about them. I just prefer to keep busy.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties with restlessness and staying seated start when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. Um... looking back, even as a girl, my report cards usually said I was bright, but... that I couldn't stay in my seat. Or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that I wore a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was ten. So... yes. It's not exactly new.\n\nDoctor: When you’re working on something, do you often have trouble keeping your attention on it?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. Um... if I have something to do, I just do it. I don't really have trouble focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose track of what you’re doing or make careless mistakes because your mind wanders?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I don't really make careless mistakes. Um... I keep track of what I'm doing just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act without thinking first, even when you know you probably should stop and consider it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I usually think things through. I end up just feeling a bit embarrassed about it all, rather than enjoying the time with them.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or family members say you had trouble paying attention or seemed easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't know about distracted, exactly. Um... my report cards back then usually said I was bright, but... that I just couldn't stay in my seat. Or wait my turn, I suppose. But... that's about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling worried or anxious about many different things most days?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Um... just the normal amount, I suppose. I don't really spend my days worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying close attention to details or make careless mistakes when doing tasks?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I have to pay pretty close attention to things, grading papers and all. So... no, I don't think I make careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have these restlessness and getting-up habits been present in more than one setting, like both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't really think so. Um... at home, I just go about my evening, so I haven't really noticed it there. Just... what I mentioned before. I wouldn't call it a habit.\n\nDoctor: Have these restlessness and getting-up habits caused problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I suppose... well, it has caused some friction. People tend to lose patience with me when I can't just sit still. It happens at staff meetings... and at my book club. I end up just feeling a bit embarrassed about it all, rather than enjoying the time with them.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or family members say you had trouble paying attention or seemed easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't know about distracted, exactly. Um... my report cards back then usually said I was bright, but... that I just couldn't stay in my seat. Or wait my turn, I suppose. My mother used to joke about it... she'd say I wore a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was ten. But... yes, I suppose that was something they noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention when reading, listening, or doing tasks?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean... I read quite a lot. And I can listen just fine when I need to. I don't really have a problem focusing on things like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose track of what you're doing because your mind wanders?\n\nPatient: No... I wouldn't say so. My mind doesn't really wander like that. I usually know what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this restlessness in more than one setting, such as both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure. I haven't paid much attention to that, to be honest. I suppose I haven't really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave tasks unfinished because you get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I usually finish what I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this need to get up and move around happens in more than one setting, like both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean... I'm on my feet a lot at the school anyway, but I wouldn't say I feel some sort of need to keep moving when I'm at home.
